"Here is **the verb “to abstract”** in a variety of world languages, including **meaning, typical usage**, and **IPA pronunciation**.
Since “abstract” is a specialized verb (meaning *to extract, summarize, or remove something conceptually*), some languages use *loanwords* or *phrases* rather than a native single verb.
---
# 🌍 **“Abstract” as a Verb in World Languages**
## **1. English**
* **abstract** (to summarize; to extract)
* **IPA:** /æbˈstrækt/ (verb)
---
## **2. Spanish**
* **abstraer** (to abstract; to extract conceptually)
* **IPA:** /aβs̺tɾaˈeɾ/
* Common usage: *abstraer una idea* (“to abstract an idea”)
* **resumir** also means “to summarize,” depending on meaning.
---
## **3. French**
* **abstraire** (to abstract conceptually)
* **IPA:** /apstʁɛʁ/
* Rare in everyday use; used in philosophy.
* **résumer** = “to summarize.”
---
## **4. German**
* **abstrahieren** (to abstract, to generalize)
* **IPA:** /apstʁaˈhiːʁən/
---
## **5. Italian**
* **astrarre** (to abstract)
* **IPA:** /asˈtrare/
* **riassumere** = “to summarize.”
---
## **6. Portuguese**
* **abstrair** (to abstract)
* **IPA:** /abʃtɾaˈiɾ/
* Often used in philosophical or academic contexts.
---
## **7. Russian**
* **абстрагировать** (abstragírovat’) — to abstract, detach, conceptualize
* **IPA:** /əbstɾɐˈɡʲirovətʲ/
---
## **8. Greek**
* **αφαιρώ** (*afairó*) — to remove/extract
IPA: /afeˈro/
* **αφαιρώ νοητικά** (“to abstract mentally”)
* **περιγράφω συνοπτικά** (“to summarize abstractly”)
---
## **9. Chinese (Mandarin)**
Chinese uses verbs depending on meaning:
### *To summarize:*
* **摘要 (zhāiyào)** — summarize, abstract
**IPA:** /ʈ͡ʂáɪ̯jâʊ̯/
### *To extract conceptually:*
* **抽象化 (chōuxiànghuà)** — to abstract
**IPA:** /ʈ͡ʂʰóu̯˥˥ ɕjâŋ˥˩ xwâ/
---
## **10. Japanese**
### “To abstract”:
* **抽象化する (chūshōka suru)**
**IPA:** /t͡ɕɯːɕoːka sɯɾɯ/
### “To summarize”:
* **要約する (yōyaku suru)**
**IPA:** /joːjakɯ sɯɾɯ/
---
## **11. Korean**
### “To summarize”:
* **요약하다 (yoyak-hada)**
**IPA:** /jo̞jak̚hada/
### “To abstract (conceptually)”:
* **추상화하다 (chusanghwa-hada)**
**IPA:** /tɕʰus̟aŋβwa̠hada/
---
## **12. Arabic**
* **يُلَخِّصُ (yulakhkhiṣu)** — to summarize
**IPA:** /ju.la.xːi.sˤu/
* **يُجَرِّد (yujarrid)** — to abstract (remove, strip away)
**IPA:** /ju.d͡ʒar.rid/
---
## **13. Hindi**
* **सारांश निकालना (sārānś nikālnā)** — to abstract/summarize
**IPA:** /sɑːˈrɑːnʃ nɪˈkaːlnaː/
* **अमूर्त करना (amūrt karnā)** — to abstract conceptually
**IPA:** /əˈmuːrt kərnaː/
---
## **14. Turkish**
* **soyutlamak** — to abstract
**IPA:** /soˈjutɫamak/
* **özetlemek** — to summarize
**IPA:** /øˈzetɫemek/
---
## **15. Swedish**
* **abstrahera** — to abstract
**IPA:** /abstrɑˈheːra/
---

verbEnglB1.use::
"The verb "use" has several related meanings, but at its core, it refers to the action of employing something for a specific purpose. Here are the main meanings:
1. **To employ or operate something to achieve a purpose**
- Example: "She **uses** a calculator to solve math problems."
2. **To consume or deplete resources**
- Example: "We **used** all the paper in the printer."
3. **To take advantage of someone or something for personal gain**
- Example: "He felt **used** after they only contacted him for favors."
4. **To apply a method, idea, or concept**
- Example: "They **use** a new strategy to improve efficiency."
5. **To indicate habitual behavior (in older or formal contexts, often with "used to")**
- Example: "She **used to** go jogging every morning."
Each meaning revolves around the core idea of putting something to work for a specific function, benefit, or outcome."

who::
"Excellent question. The word for "who" is a fundamental interrogative pronoun, and its forms across language families reveal fascinating patterns of common ancestry, sound change, and grammatical structure.
Here’s a breakdown of "who" across major language families, focusing on the **interrogative pronoun** (who?) and sometimes the relative pronoun (who/that).
### 1. **Indo-European (The clearest example of reconstruction)**
This family shows the most famous and well-documented connections.
* **Proto-Indo-European (PIE):** The reconstructed root is ***kʷo-* or *kʷi-***. This was part of a broader interrogative system: *kʷo-* (masculine/neuter), *kʷih₂* (feminine).
* **Examples:**
* **Latin:** *quis* (who?)
* **Sanskrit:** *kaḥ* (who?)
* **Greek:** *τίς (tís)* (who?)
* **Germanic:** English *who*, German *wer*, Dutch *wie*. English "who" comes from Old English *hwā*, from Proto-Germanic ***hwaz***, all from PIE *kʷo-*.
* **Slavic:** Russian *кто (kto)*, Polish *kto*, from Proto-Slavic ***kъto***.
* **Celtic:** Irish *cé*, Welsh *pwy* (showing a *kʷ* -> *p* sound change, also seen in some Italic dialects).
* **Indo-Iranian:** Persian *کِی* (ki), Hindi *कौन (kaun)*.
**Key Takeaway:** The similarity between Latin *quis*, Greek *tís*, English *who*, and Russian *kto* is not coincidence but direct inheritance from PIE ***kʷo-***.
### 2. **Uralic**
* **Proto-Uralic:** The reconstructed root is ***ke-* or *ki-***.
* **Examples:**
* **Finnish:** *kuka*
* **Estonian:** *kes*
* **Hungarian:** *ki*
* **Sami:** *gi*
### 3. **Altaic (A controversial proposed family)**
Note: The existence of the Altaic family is widely disputed by modern linguists. These are often treated as separate families (Turkic, Mongolic, Tungusic).
* **Turkic:** Turkish *kim*, Azerbaijani *kim*, Uzbek *kim*.
* **Mongolic:** Mongolian *хэн (khen)*.
* **Tungusic:** Evenki *хин (hin)*.
* **Possible Proto-Altaic (theoretical):** ***ka-* or *ki-***.
### 4. **Sino-Tibetan**
This family often uses interrogative pronouns that also function as general question particles.
* **Sinitic (Chinese):** 谁 (*shéi* / *shuí* in Mandarin). In Old Chinese, it may have been related to a root like ***djər***.
* **Tibeto-Burman:**
* **Tibetan:** སུ (*su*)
* **Burmese:** ဘယ်သူ (*bhay su* – literally "which person")
### 5. **Afro-Asiatic**
* **Semitic:**
* **Proto-Semitic:** ***man-*** (likely).
* **Arabic:** مَنْ (*man*)
* **Hebrew:** מִי (*mi*)
* **Akkadian:** *mannum*
* **Other branches:** Often use *m*-based forms (e.g., Egyptian *m*).
### 6. **Austronesian (Very diverse)**
Forms vary widely, often meaning "person" or "which person."
* **Malay/Indonesian:** *siapa*
* **Tagalog:** *sino*
* **Hawaiian:** *wai* (note the *w*, a potential link to other forms)
* **Proto-Austronesian (reconstructed):** Likely ***sai* and *-nu* (as in *i-sai* "who")**.
### 7. **Niger-Congo**
Extreme diversity, but many languages in the large **Bantu** subgroup share a common form.
* **Proto-Bantu:** ***-ní*** (as a prefix or with a noun class prefix).
* **Examples:**
* **Swahili:** *nani* (from *na- + -ni*)
* **Zulu:** *ubani*
* **Shona:** *ndiani*
### 8. **Isolates (Languages with no known living relatives)**
* **Basque:** *nor*
* **Korean:** 누구 (*nugu*) (Note: Korean is sometimes linked to Altaic, but this is disputed).
* **Japanese:** 誰 (*dare*) (Japanese's relationship to other languages is unproven).
### **Linguistic Patterns and Observations:**
1. **The K/M Alternation:** One of the most striking patterns in historical linguistics is the split between languages that use a **kʷ-/k-/qu-** sound for "who" (most of Indo-European, Uralic, Altaic) and those that use an **m-** sound (Semitic, many others). This is likely a very ancient areal feature or a case of convergent development, not proof of a single ancestor.
2. **Stability:** Interrogative words like "who" and "what" are among the most resistant to change in a language, making them excellent for tracing deep family relationships.
3. **Suppletion:** In some languages, "who" has completely different forms for interrogative and relative functions (e.g., English *who* vs. *that*), while in others it's the same (e.g., German *wer* can be both, though *der* is more common as relative).
In summary, tracing "who" through language families is like following a core piece of DNA. Its forms, especially in ancient families like Indo-European, provide some of the strongest evidence for genetic relationships between languages. The global map shows a fascinating but complex distribution of **k/q-** and **m-** based forms."
[{2025-12-22 retrieved} https://chat.deepseek.com/a/chat/s/7a172645-5495-4bc0-8af1-b1933358afc6]

* McsEngl.who@EnglWord,

when::
"The word ***when*** has a long Germanic history tied to time and questioning.
### Etymology of **when**
* **Old English:** *hwænne* — meaning “at what time” or “when”
* **Proto-Germanic:** **hwan(n)ē* — a temporal interrogative (“when?”)
* **Proto-Indo-European (PIE):** **kʷo-* — a root meaning “who, what,” which also produced many question words
### How it evolved
* The original **hw-** sound in Old English (*hwænne*) later simplified to **wh-** in Modern English.
* It belongs to the same family as **what**, **where**, **why**, and **who**, all derived from the PIE interrogative root **kʷo-*.
* Over time, **when** expanded from a purely interrogative word (“At what time?”) to also function as:
* a **relative adverb** (“the day **when** we met”)
* a **conjunction** (“Call me **when** you arrive”)
### Key idea
At its core, **when** literally means **“at which time?”**, a meaning it has preserved remarkably well for over a thousand years.
If you’d like, I can also compare it with **why / where / while**, or show how its usage shifted syntactically over time."
[{2025-12-23 retrieved} https://chatgpt.com/c/694a790c-6a2c-832a-8538-f6b8c0ba90dd]
